Use Case 1: implementation runbook

Step by step, phase by phase: what the team does, in which tool, with which file, how it is checked, reviewed and tested before a step is closed, and how each step feeds the next. Written for a team doing this for the first time. The design behind it is in UseCase1.html.

  • Phase 0 environment
  • Phase 1 artifacts: built · 5 open steps
  • Phases 2–6 planned
  • Synthetic data only

Synthetic. "Investor A Servicing Guide v2025.1 / v2026.1", every application and every borrower are invented. Regulatory points follow 12 CFR 1024.41 and must be confirmed by compliance before real use.

1How to use this runbook#

This runbook turns the Use Case 1 design into steps a team can follow one at a time. Sections 1 to 6 (this part) cover the way of working, the environment (Phase 0) and the artifacts (Phase 1); they are written from the real files in usecase1/ and from checks that were actually run. Later sections cover Phases 2 to 6, which are plans.

1.1 Who this is for

A capable development team doing this for the first time: comfortable with Python, SQL, git and PowerShell, but new to VocBench 3 (a web-based editor for taxonomies (SKOS); subject-matter experts maintain the controlled words here, with review before publishing), Protégé (a desktop editor for ontologies (OWL) from Stanford; the knowledge engineer edits the model and the requirements here), SHACL (the W3C language for rules a data graph must satisfy; the validation report lists each violation) and OWL (the W3C ontology language: classes, properties and their rules). You do not need to know any of them before you start: every card says which file to open, in which tool, what to click or run, and what you should see.

  • Read section 2 (the big picture) once, so every file has a place in your head.
  • Read section 4 (how every step runs) once: every card follows that loop.
  • Then work card by card. A card is finished only when its ✅ Done when list holds.

1.2 How a step card reads

Every step is one card with the same fields, in the same order:

FieldWhat it tells you
HeaderStep number, title and status badge.
PurposeWhy the step exists: what would go wrong without it.
WhoThe role that does it and the roles that take part (section 3).
InputsFiles (name, extension, folder) and tools that must exist before you start.
DoNumbered actions, click by click or command by command. 👤 marks an action a person does in a tool; ⚙ marks a command you run.
OutputEvery file the step creates or changes, with its folder.
⚙ VerifyThe exact check command and the output you should see (real captured output, trimmed).
🔍 ReviewThe questions the independent review (a fresh reviewer (person or sub-agent) checks the output against the design and lists findings; they do not edit) must answer.
Feeds into →Which later step reads the output, and how.
✅ Done whenThe conditions that close the step (its gate (the agreed exit check of a step or phase; work moves on only when every gate item holds)).

Legend. 👤 a person acts in a tool · ⚙ an automatic check (a command whose output is compared with the expected output) · 🔍 an independent review · ✅ the gate: the conditions that close the step.

Status badgeMeaning
doneBuilt, checked and independently reviewed; findings fixed.
open · action neededSomebody has to act now; the card says what is left.
partly doneSome of the Do list is finished; the card says which part.
plannedA later phase; names and outputs are planned, nothing exists yet.

1.3 Conventions

  • Paths are relative to the repo root, e.g. usecase1/ontology/uc1-core.ttl. On the authoring machine the root is C:\VSquareThoughts\rag-os-assistant; yours is wherever you cloned it. Tool installs live outside the repo, under C:\Tools\.
  • Commands are PowerShell 7 (pwsh), run from the repo root. Check with $PSVersionTable.PSVersion (7.x). Forward slashes in paths work in PowerShell.
  • Python tools come through uv (the Python package and environment manager used in this repo; `uv run --with …` fetches tools into a throw-away environment): uv run --no-project --with rdflib==7.6.0 … puts the pinned libraries into a throw-away environment. Nothing is installed into the project.
  • Behind a TLS-inspecting proxy (a corporate proxy or an antivirus web shield) add --native-tls (a uv flag that trusts the Windows certificate store; needed when a proxy or antivirus re-signs HTTPS traffic) right after uv run, e.g. uv run --native-tls --no-project ….
  • Menus are written Global Data Management → Load Data. These labels were checked on VocBench 15.1.2 and Protégé 5.6.9; where a label was not checked, the card describes the function instead of guessing a menu path.
  • Expected output is real output captured on 2026-10-07 and trimmed with “…”. Output that cannot be captured yet is marked expected (not yet captured).
  • New words. The first time a term appears in a section it is shown in bold with a short note in brackets; after that, hover the dotted word to see the note again. The glossary (section 17) has all of them.

1.4 Where things live

OpenWhat it isOpen it when
usecase1/README.mdThe naming contract (usecase1/README.md: every prefix, concept id, class, property, table and column name; a name changes there first, then everywhere): every prefix, concept id, class, property, table and column.Before you name anything. A name changes there first, then everywhere.
usecase1/CHANGES.mdThe ledger (CHANGES.md) (the list of every Use Case 1 artifact and how to remove it, so the use case stays removable): every artifact, its phase, status and how to remove it.Whenever a step adds or changes an artifact (same pull request).
docs/UseCase1.htmlThe design: why the model looks as it does, the worked example, the implementation plan (section 14).When a card says “as the design says”, or a review needs the intent.

2The big picture#

2.1 Three layers and an engine

Use Case 1 decides whether a mortgage loss-mitigation application is complete under the guide version that governs it, and lists exactly what is missing. Three kinds of knowledge are kept apart, each in its own files and owned by different people, and one engine combines them:

LayerAnswersLives inEdited in
Facts: the semantic model (the relational tables (uc1_*) that hold the facts of each case in business terms)What is true about this case?usecase1/sql/*.sql → SQLite (a single-file database built into Python; the local home of the uc1_* tables (.data/uc1.db)) (.data/uc1.db) or PostgreSQL (the production-grade database RAG-OS uses in Azure; we prove our SQL also runs there, in a separate uc1 schema)text editor (VS Code (the code editor used for the text artifacts (SQL, SHACL, YAML, Turtle)))
Words: the taxonomy (the controlled words of the domain, arranged in a hierarchy with synonyms)What do we call things, and what is a kind of what?usecase1/taxonomy/*.skos.ttlVocBench 3 (a web-based editor for taxonomies (SKOS); subject-matter experts maintain the controlled words here, with review before publishing)
Rules: the ontology (the formal model of the domain's things and relationships, precise enough for software to check) + SHACL (the W3C language for rules a data graph must satisfy; the validation report lists each violation)What does guide version V require, when, how recent?usecase1/ontology/*.ttlProtégé (a desktop editor for ontologies (OWL) from Stanford; the knowledge engineer edits the model and the requirements here) (rules); VS Code (SHACL, engineer only)
Engine (Phase 2, planned)Which requirements are missing for this application?the bridge (the planned code that turns uc1_* rows into an RDF graph, adding derived values) + pySHACL + the completeness template (the single SHACL-SPARQL rule (uc1-requirements.shapes.ttl) that checks every requirement of the governing version)code; today the golden cases (a hand-written test application with the answer fixed in advance by the compliance view) and run_golden.py stand in for it

The layers never call each other. They meet only in the completeness engine (bridge + pySHACL + template: decides which requirements are missing for one application), which is deterministic: the same files and the same rows always give the same missing items. Language models are used only at the edges (recognising a document's type, drafting the letter) and never decide completeness.

2.2 Artifact catalogue

One row per real artifact today. “Source of truth” says whether people edit the file (yes), a tool writes it (generated), or it is rebuilt from other files (derived). Every file is written in Turtle (.ttl) (a readable text format for RDF; every ontology, taxonomy and golden file here is Turtle), SQL, YAML, XML, Markdown or Python.

FileFolderWhat it isMade inOpened / edited inSource of truth?Consumed byChecked by

2.4 How the artifacts come together

Each layer hands the next one something it can check. The taxonomy fixes the words, the requirement files point at those words, the facts are described with the same words, and one SHACL template compares facts with rules. Follow the worked example, application APP-1001 (golden case G01):

  1. Words. The taxonomy has uc1tx:ProfitAndLoss: prefLabel “Profit and loss statement”, altLabel “P&L” and “income statement”, notation (a concept's short code (e.g. ProfitAndLoss); here it equals the IRI's local name and the RAG-OS facet value id) ProfitAndLoss, broader (the parent link in a taxonomy hierarchy (Paystub broader Income)) Income evidence.
  2. Rules. investor-a-2026.1.ttl says requirement ia26:R4: document type ProfitAndLoss, applies when employmentType = SelfEmployed, recency maxAgeDays 90 and signatureRequired true, cites §4.3.2. v2025.1 only asked for 120 days.
  3. Facts. APP-1001 was received Fri 2026-02-06 15:30 UTC; its one borrower is self-employed; document D3 is classified ProfitAndLoss, dated 2025-10-31 and unsigned. The bridge (today: the golden graph) adds the derived values (a value computed from stored facts when needed (document age, governing version), never stored itself): ageAtReceiptDays 98 and governingVersion v2026.1.
  4. Check. pySHACL (a Python library that checks a data graph against SHACL rules and reports every violation; it is the completeness engine's rule runner) runs the template: R4 applies (self-employed) and D3 fails it (98 > 90, unsigned). R5 fails too (only the December bank statement; two months are required).
  5. Answer. Missing R4 and R5, each with its guide section; the notice is due Fri 2026-02-13 (the 5th business day (any day except Saturday, Sunday or a legal public holiday (Reg X counting)) after receipt). Phase 4 adds a section-level citation into the guide text and Phase 5 the letter.

3Roles and responsibilities#

3.1 The six roles

One person can hold several roles, except that the reviewer of a step is never its builder.

RoleWhat they do in Use Case 1Tools they use
SME (subject-matter expert)Owns the meaning: the words (labels, synonyms, definitions), the content of each guide version's requirements, and the scenarios of the golden cases. Signs off words and requirements.VocBench 3 (a web-based editor for taxonomies (SKOS); subject-matter experts maintain the controlled words here, with review before publishing) in the browser (edits); Protégé to read requirements; the design page
KE (knowledge engineer)Builds and maintains the ontology core and the requirement files; keeps the model inside OWL 2 DL (the decidable profile of OWL that reasoners like HermiT accept; our files must stay inside it); answers modelling questions.Protégé (a desktop editor for ontologies (OWL) from Stanford; the knowledge engineer edits the model and the requirements here) with HermiT (a reasoner bundled with Protégé: software that checks the ontology's logic for contradictions; we use it to prove the model is consistent); VS Code for Turtle; ROBOT (a command-line ontology tool; we used it once to confirm the files are valid OWL 2 DL)
EngineerWrites the SQL, the SHACL shapes, the golden graphs and the checks; later the engine and the Admin page. Runs every ⚙ check.VS Code, PowerShell 7, uv (the Python package and environment manager used in this repo; `uv run --with …` fetches tools into a throw-away environment), git, Docker Desktop (runs containers on Windows; we use it only to start a local PostgreSQL for the portability proof)
ReviewerPerforms the independent review (a fresh reviewer (person or sub-agent) checks the output against the design and lists findings; they do not edit) of each step: reads the output against the design and the card's questions, records findings (one problem a reviewer records: severity, location, evidence, the fix asked for and what happened to it), never edits.git diff (shows line by line what changed in a file since the last commit; the reviewer's main evidence), the checks (read-only), the design page
ComplianceConfirms the regulatory reading: the expected answers in expected.yaml, the 5-business-day clock, later the letter. Signs off at the artifact gate (the Phase 1 exit check: every artifact proven by its tool and checks, and signed off by its owners, before any engine code).the design page, expected.yaml, golden scenario titles
Product ownerAccountable for each phase gate; decides scope and open items; keeps the ledger honest.usecase1/CHANGES.md, the pull requests, this runbook

Supporting roles

Later cards (mostly Phases 2–6) also name these roles. They are specialists consulted for one kind of change, not owners of an artifact; one person may cover several of them.

RoleResponsibility in Use Case 1
Tech leadOwns the engineering approach; runs and signs off the technical gate evidence (clean-clone run, test results) and signs off the Phase 1 artifact gate with the SME, compliance and KE.
Platform engineerReviews packaging and deployment changes: the Dockerfile, docker-compose, settings and environment variables in Deployment.md.
Security reviewerReviews authorisation (the admin role on every Use Case 1 endpoint), input validation and the four-eyes rule.
Privacy reviewerChecks that personal data stays minimal: no PII in the engine graph, in prompts or in logs.
Database reviewerReviews SQL and schema changes: types, constraints, indexes, and that the uc1 schema stays separate and removable.
Front-end engineerBuilds the Admin workbench views in chat-ui/ (Phase 3).
SME leadSpeaks for the SMEs on taxonomy decisions (new concepts, group concepts, facet names) and on the shadow-run comparison.
Access ownerDecides the access tags (department, region, clearance) on the synthetic guide documents in RAG-OS (Phase 4).

3.2 RACI per phase

RACI (per activity: who is Responsible (does it), Accountable (approves it), Consulted (asked first) and Informed (told afterwards)). One A per phase: the product owner. “C (signs off …)” marks a consulted role whose approval is still required before the gate closes. At the Phase 1 artifact gate the product owner is accountable and the SME, compliance, KE and tech lead sign off.

PhaseSMEKEEngineerReviewerComplianceProduct owner
0 EnvironmentR (VocBench account)R (Protégé)R (Java, uv, VocBench server, Docker)IIA
1 ArtifactsR (words, requirement content); C (signs off)R (core, requirement files)R (SQL, SHACL, golden graphs, checks)C (every step)C (signs off expected.yaml and the clock rule)A (artifact gate)
2 Engine codeIC (bridge mapping)RCIA
3 Admin workbench (the planned 'Use Case 1' page in RAG-OS Admin for reviewing, validating and processing (not editing))C (uses it to validate and evaluate)CRC (second approver of four-eyes publish (a change goes live only after a second, different person approves it))IA
4 CitationsC (guide text and sections)IRCC (citations are evidence)A
5 Letter + verifierC (wording)IRCC (signs off the letter and clock tests)A
6 Pilot + removal rehearsalR (compares the shadow run (running the engine on past applications next to the people who handled them, to measure precision before switching over) with the handlers)IR (runs it; removal rehearsal (following the ledger's removal steps on a scratch copy to prove Use Case 1 leaves no trace))C (no trace left)C (accepts precision)A (go / no-go)

4How every step runs#

Every card, in every phase, runs the same loop. It is deliberately boring: the same evidence in the same place each time, so nobody has to ask whether a step was really checked.

4.1 The loop

  1. 👤 Build. The owner produces the card's outputs in the tool the card names (VocBench, Protégé, VS Code).
  2. ⚙ Check. Run the card's Verify command and compare the output with the expected output, line by line. Any FAIL sends you back to build. Never edit a check to make it pass; if the contract really changed, change usecase1/README.md first, then the check, in the same pull request.
  3. 🔍 Independent review (a fresh reviewer (person or sub-agent) checks the output against the design and lists findings; they do not edit). Someone who did not build the step (a colleague, or a fresh AI sub-agent with no memory of the build) reads the output against the design and the card's 🔍 questions and records findings.
  4. Fix. The owner fixes every BLOCKING (a finding that makes the output wrong or unsafe; the step cannot close until it is fixed and re-checked) and SHOULD-FIX (a real weakness that does not break the output today; fixed in the same step unless the owner records why not) finding, or records with the product owner why a SHOULD-FIX waits. Each NIT (a cosmetic point (wording, order, spelling); fixed if cheap, never blocks a step) is the owner's call.
  5. ⚙ Re-check. Run run_all.py (the one command that runs every Phase 1 check and prints PASS/FAIL per area), not only the step's own check: a fix in one artifact can break another (that is what check_consistency.py is for).
  6. ✅ Gate (the agreed exit check of a step or phase; work moves on only when every gate item holds). Every Done when condition holds and the reviewer confirms each finding's disposition.
  7. Ledger (the list of every Use Case 1 artifact and how to remove it, so the use case stays removable). Add or update the row in usecase1/CHANGES.md in the same change, then merge the pull request (a proposed change in git that reviewers approve before it is merged).

4.2 Definition of Done

The Definition of Done (the conditions every step must meet before it can be closed) for every step:

  • Every output named on the card exists, with the exact name and folder the naming contract (usecase1/README.md: every prefix, concept id, class, property, table and column name; a name changes there first, then everywhere) gives.
  • Its ⚙ Verify output matches the expected output, and run_all.py prints ALL PASS.
  • An independent review is recorded; no BLOCKING finding is open; every SHOULD-FIX is fixed or has a recorded reason.
  • Files edited in a tool (VocBench export, Protégé save) were inspected with git diff (shows line by line what changed in a file since the last commit; the reviewer's main evidence) before commit.
  • The ledger row is current, and no product file (src/rag_os, chat-ui, config/, migrations, pyproject.toml) changed unless the card says so and the ledger lists it.
  • Required sign-offs (a named owner's recorded approval (in the pull request or the review record) that an artifact says what they mean) are recorded in the pull request.

4.3 Recording a review

The reviewer writes one table per step, in the pull request description (or as a review comment), with one row per finding. Severity is one of BLOCKING, SHOULD-FIX or NIT. Evidence is something another person can re-check: a line, a command and its output, a section of the design.

SeverityLocationEvidenceFixDisposition
BLOCKINGuc1-core.ttl: every date property (e.g. effectiveFromDate); the version files and golden graphsxsd:date is not in the OWL 2 datatype map; HermiT (in Protégé and in check_ontology.py) refuses the import closureWrite dates as xsd:dateTime at midnight (2026-01-01T00:00:00); SQL keeps real DATE columns and the bridge converts; add the check “only OWL 2 datatypes”fixed; re-checked (HermiT consistent)
SHOULD-FIXuc1_create.sql: the evaluation item had one satisfying-document columnOne item can need several documents: two bank statements satisfy R5Link table uc1_evaluation_item_document (composite primary key)fixed; re-checked
SHOULD-FIXuc1_create.sql: classification_confidenceThe declared type was not portable to both SQLite and PostgreSQLDeclare it DOUBLE PRECISION; the bridge converts to the xsd:decimal the shapes expectfixed; carried as an open item for the bridge
SHOULD-FIXuc1_borrower: tax_id_last4Personal data that no requirement or check needsDrop the column (data minimisation)fixed
SHOULD-FIXuc1-taxonomy.seed.skos.ttl: SKOS terms used without declarationsUndeclared annotation terms take the import closure outside OWL 2 DL (the decidable profile of OWL that reasoners like HermiT accept; our files must stay inside it)Declare the SKOS and DCT (a W3C-recognised vocabulary for titles and descriptions of files and vocabularies) terms in the taxonomy file; confirm with ROBOT (a command-line ontology tool; we used it once to confirm the files are valid OWL 2 DL) validate-profile --profile DLfixed; ROBOT: closure in profile

These are real Phase 1 findings, shown as the table should look. Other Phase 1 findings fixed the same way: synonym clashes in the taxonomy, missing functional (a property that may have at most one value; lets the reasoner catch e.g. an item that is both Met and Missing) declarations, and an axiom allow-list (a check that only permits agreed kinds of statements in the core, so a stray 'functional' or 'equivalent' axiom can't sneak in) so the core carries only the axiom kinds the design uses.

4.4 Why reviews are independent and read-only

  • Independent, because the builder's blind spots are exactly where the defects are. A reviewer who did not build the step reads what is there, not what was meant. In Phase 1 every built step was reviewed by a fresh reviewer with no access to the builder's reasoning, and every finding above came from that.
  • Read-only, because a reviewer who edits becomes a second builder: the fix is then unreviewed, and the finding disappears from the record. The reviewer writes the finding; the owner fixes it; the re-check proves it.
  • Recorded, because the findings table is the audit trail. For a regulated process the question “who checked this, against what, and what changed?” must have a written answer.

5Phase 0: environment setup#

Phase 0 installs the tools every later step uses. Each machine that edits or checks Use Case 1 needs 0.1, 0.2 and 0.6; SMEs and the KE also need 0.3 and 0.4; only the engineer running 1.7b needs 0.5. 0.8 is done once for the team, before anyone edits a file in a tool; 0.9 is optional (the KE or the engineer who runs the OWL 2 DL profile check). All installs live outside the repo and are listed in the ledger with their removal.

0.1Java 21 (Temurin)done
Purpose
This card installs Temurin JDK 21 (a free Java runtime from Eclipse Adoptium; VocBench needs Java 17 or later). VocBench's server (Semantic Turkey (the server VocBench runs on; it stores each project's data and is started by bin\semanticturkey.bat)) is a Java program and needs Java 17 or later. check_ontology.py starts HermiT (a reasoner bundled with Protégé: software that checks the ontology's logic for contradictions; we use it to prove the model is consistent) through owlready2 (a Python library that can run HermiT from a script; our checks use it for an automatic consistency test), which also needs Java, and ROBOT (0.9) is a Java .jar. Protégé brings its own Java, so this install is for the rest.
Who
Engineer, on every machine that runs VocBench or the checks.

    powershell
    [Environment]::SetEnvironmentVariable('JAVA_HOME', 'C:\Program Files\Eclipse Adoptium\jdk-21.0.11.10-hotspot', 'User')
Output

0.3VocBench 15.1.2done
Purpose
VocBench 3 (a web-based editor for taxonomies (SKOS); subject-matter experts maintain the controlled words here, with review before publishing) is where SMEs maintain the taxonomy, with history and a validation workflow (VocBench's propose → approve process: an edit stays pending until a validator accepts it); its export is the taxonomy's source of truth. It is a local web server plus a browser app.
Who
Engineer installs and starts it; the first user to register becomes its administrator (the engineer or the product owner).
Inputs
  • Java 21 (0.1).
  • A browser.

Do
  1. 👤 Download vocbench3-15.1.2-full.zip from bitbucket.org/art-uniroma2/vocbench3/downloads. The “full” zip contains the Semantic Turkey server and the VocBench web app together.
  2. ⚙ Unzip it. The zip's top folder is semanticturkey-15.1.2; rename it so the install path matches this runbook:
    powershell
    Expand-Archive -Path "$HOME\Downloads\vocbench3-15.1.2-full.zip" -DestinationPath C:\Tools
Rename-Item C:\Tools\semanticturkey-15.1.2 vocbench3-15.1.2-full
Get-ChildItem C:\Tools\vocbench3-15.1.2-full          # bin, config, lib, log, plugins

    powershell

  6. 👤 First run only: register the first user. That user becomes the administrator. Use a real e-mail address as the login; keep the password in the team's password manager.
  7. 👤 Keep the console window open while you work: closing it stops the server.
  8. 👤 To stop VocBench: click into the console window and press Ctrl+C (answer Y if asked to terminate the batch job). Projects are kept.
Output

0.8Branch and commitopen · action needed
Purpose
Put Use Case 1 under version control on its own branch (a separate line of work in git; Phase 1 work lives on a branch until its pull request is merged) before anyone edits a file in a tool. git diff (shows line by line what changed in a file since the last commit; the reviewer's main evidence) and git restore compare with the last commit, so they only work on committed files: on an untracked file, 1.1u cannot show what the VocBench export changed and 1.2r cannot undo its practice edit. Reviewers also review diffs, in a pull request (a proposed change in git that reviewers approve before it is merged), not folders.
Who
Engineer (once, for the team); the tech lead opens the Phase 1 pull request.
Inputs
  • 0.7 (the tree to commit).
  • git, and the repo at the current main branch.
  • Today's state: usecase1/ and the three docs/ files are untracked.
Do
  1. ⚙ Create the Phase 1 branch:
    powershell
    git switch -c uc1/phase1
  2. ⚙ Stage Use Case 1 by name. Name the paths rather than git add .: other untracked files in the working copy (for example under KnowledgeBaseDocuments/) are not part of Use Case 1. .data/ and __pycache__/ are git-ignored, so they stay out.
    powershell
    git add usecase1 docs/UseCase1.html docs/UseCase1_DetailedImplementation.html docs/use-case-1.md
  3. ⚙ Commit:
    powershell
    git commit -m "Use Case 1: phase 1 artifacts"
  4. ⚙ Push the branch so others, and the 1.8 clean-clone run, can fetch it; then open a draft pull request for Phase 1 (every later Phase 1 step adds commits to it):
    powershell
    git push -u origin uc1/phase1
Output
  • Branch uc1/phase1 with one commit holding usecase1/ (32 files) and docs/UseCase1.html, docs/UseCase1_DetailedImplementation.html, docs/use-case-1.md; pushed to origin; a draft pull request.
⚙ Verify
powershell
git status --short usecase1 docs/UseCase1*.html
git log --oneline -1

The first command must print nothing (no untracked or modified Use Case 1 file). The second shows the new commit, e.g. <hash> Use Case 1: phase 1 artifacts.

🔍 Review
  • Does the commit contain only Use Case 1 files (git show --stat HEAD)?
  • Is no product file (src/rag_os, chat-ui, config/, pyproject.toml) in it?
  • Is .data/uc1.db absent (git-ignored)?
Feeds into →
1.1u and 1.2r (the baseline that git diff and git restore compare with); 1.8 (the clean clone of uc1/phase1 and the Phase 1 pull request).
✅ Done when
  • git status --short usecase1 docs/UseCase1*.html prints nothing; the branch is pushed.
0.9ROBOT (optional, for the OWL 2 DL profile check)done
Purpose
ROBOT (a command-line ontology tool; we used it once to confirm the files are valid OWL 2 DL) checks that an ontology and everything it imports stay inside OWL 2 DL (the decidable profile of OWL that reasoners like HermiT accept; our files must stay inside it). HermiT (in check_ontology.py) proves consistency but not the profile, and run_all.py does not run ROBOT, so this is a manual check for 1.2 and the 1.8 gate.
Who
KE or engineer.
Inputs
  • Java 21 (0.1).
Do
  1. 👤 Download robot.jar of release v1.9.8 from github.com/ontodev/robot/releases.
  2. ⚙ Put it in C:\Tools\robot:
    powershell
    New-Item -ItemType Directory -Force C:\Tools\robot
Move-Item "$HOME\Downloads\robot.jar" C:\Tools\robot\robot.jar
  3. ⚙ Check the version:
    powershell
    java -jar C:\Tools\robot\robot.jar --version
  4. ⚙ Run the profile check once from the repo root (the same command 1.2 and 1.8 use):
    powershell
    # ROBOT 1.9.8 from step 0.9 (needs Java from step 0.1). --catalog makes ROBOT resolve every owl:imports through
# catalog-v001.xml, so the whole import closure (this file + the core + the taxonomy) is checked. The report goes to the console.
java -jar C:\Tools\robot\robot.jar validate-profile --profile DL --catalog usecase1/ontology/catalog-v001.xml --input usecase1/ontology/investor-a-2026.1.ttl
Output
  • C:\Tools\robot\robot.jar. Nothing in the repo; the ledger lists ROBOT under the other local tools.
⚙ Verify

text

  • Did the profile check print “Ontology and imports closure in profile”?
Feeds into →

✅ Done when

Status: done in the sense that ROBOT 1.9.8 was used during the Phase 1 review, with exactly this command and output, from a temporary folder. On a team machine, place it in C:\Tools\robot as above.

6Phase 1: artifacts#

Goal, order and exit criteria

Goal. Build every declarative artifact of Use Case 1 (the words, the rules, the facts, the expected answers) and prove each one with its own tool and an automatic check, without writing any application code.

Why artifacts come first.

  • The people who own the meaning (SMEs, compliance) can read and sign off the words, the rules and the expected answers before engineering time is spent. A wrong rule found now costs one line; found after the engine is built it costs a release.
  • Everything is a file in git that its own tool can open: VocBench 3 reads the taxonomy, Protégé and HermiT the ontology, pySHACL the shapes, SQLite and PostgreSQL the scripts. No code is needed to prove them.
  • The golden cases become the acceptance tests of Phase 2: the engine is “done” when it reproduces expected.yaml from the database. That is test-first by construction.

Order. 1.0 → 1.1 → 1.1u (VocBench) · 1.1 → 1.2 → 1.2r (Protégé) · 1.2 → 1.4 → 1.5 → 1.6 · 1.2 → 1.3 → 1.7 → 1.7b · everything → 1.8. The 👤 tool steps (1.1u, 1.2r) need the commit of 0.8 first. They and the PostgreSQL proof (1.7b) can run in parallel once their inputs exist.

Exit criteria. The artifact gate (the Phase 1 exit check: every artifact proven by its tool and checks, and signed off by its owners, before any engine code) from the design (section 14.2), as a checklist at the end of this section: HermiT consistent and the closure in OWL 2 DL (the decidable profile of OWL that reasoners like HermiT accept; our files must stay inside it); the SKOS export round-trips; every golden case gives exactly its expected list under both guide versions and the mutation tests pass; the SQL loads on SQLite and PostgreSQL; run_all.py prints ALL PASS; the SME, compliance, KE and tech lead have signed off and the product owner closes the gate.

1.0Scaffold: folder, naming contract, ledger, check runnerdone
Purpose
One folder holds everything, so Use Case 1 can be removed without trace; every name is fixed before any file is written, so the taxonomy, ontology, SHACL, golden graphs and SQL agree; one command runs all checks. This is the scaffold (the empty structure a piece of work starts from: folders, the naming contract, the ledger and the check runner).
Who
Engineer (writes); KE and SME (agree the names); independent reviewer (a fresh reviewer (person or sub-agent) checks the output against the design and lists findings; they do not edit).
Inputs
  • docs/UseCase1.html (sections 5–8: tables, taxonomy, ontology)
  • docs/use-case-1.md (the brief)
Do
  1. 👤 Create usecase1/ with taxonomy/, ontology/, golden/, sql/ and checks/.
  2. 👤 Write usecase1/README.md, the naming contract (usecase1/README.md: every prefix, concept id, class, property, table and column name; a name changes there first, then everywhere): the seven prefixes (uc1:, uc1tx:, uc1shape:, ia:, ia25:, ia26:, app:), the taxonomy table (scheme → concept ids, labels, synonyms), every class, property (domain → range, inverse, functional, stored direction (the one side of an inverse pair actually written in data and read by the rules; the other side is inferred only)), the derived values and every table and column. Its first rule: “Change a name here first, then everywhere.”
  3. 👤 Write usecase1/CHANGES.md, the ledger (CHANGES.md) (the list of every Use Case 1 artifact and how to remove it, so the use case stays removable): one row per artifact (phase, status, “remove with”) and a numbered “Removal, in order”.
  4. 👤 Write usecase1/checks/common.py (paths, namespaces, a Report that prints PASS / FAIL lines, UTF-8 console) and usecase1/checks/run_all.py (taxonomy → ontology → SQL → golden → consistency; exit code 0 only if every block passes).
  5. ⚙ Run run_all.py. While a later step's files do not exist yet, its block reports them as missing; that is expected until the step is built.
Output
  • usecase1/README.md
  • usecase1/CHANGES.md
  • usecase1/checks/common.py
  • usecase1/checks/run_all.py

1.1Taxonomy seeddone
Purpose
Agree the words (4 concept schemes (one controlled list in the taxonomy, e.g. uc1_doc_type; it becomes one RAG-OS facet), 29 concepts (one entry of the taxonomy, e.g. Paystub), their labels, synonyms and definitions) in one reviewable file before VocBench is involved, so the VocBench project starts from content the SMEs have already approved. This file is the seed (a starter file holding the agreed first version of the taxonomy; used once to fill an empty VocBench project).
Who
SME (content); KE (SKOS (the W3C vocabulary for taxonomies: concepts, schemes, preferred/alternative labels, broader/narrower) structure); engineer (check); reviewer.
Inputs
  • The taxonomy table in usecase1/README.md; design section 6.
Do
  1. 👤 In VS Code create the Turtle (.ttl) (a readable text format for RDF; every ontology, taxonomy and golden file here is Turtle) file usecase1/taxonomy/uc1-taxonomy.seed.skos.ttl with the header <https://rag-os.example/uc1/taxonomy> a owl:Ontology (+ dct:title, owl:versionInfo (a version annotation in an ontology header; each requirements file carries its guide version label there) "seed-1", a comment that the content is synthetic).
  2. 👤 Declare the SKOS and DCT (Dublin Core terms) (a W3C-recognised vocabulary for titles and descriptions of files and vocabularies) terms the file uses (skos:Concept a owl:Class, skos:prefLabel a owl:AnnotationProperty, …) so this file and every ontology that imports it stay OWL 2 DL.
  3. 👤 Write one scheme per facet: uc1tx:uc1_doc_type (hierarchical), uc1_employment_type, uc1_hardship_reason, uc1_workout_option, each with skos:prefLabel@en, skos:notation and skos:hasTopConcept.
  4. 👤 Write each concept: IRI (a web-style global identifier for a thing, e.g. https://rag-os.example/uc1/taxonomy#Paystub; like a primary key that is unique worldwide) uc1tx:<id>; skos:notation "<id>" (plain literal, equal to the IRI's local name); exactly one prefLabel (a concept's one preferred name per language)@en. Add synonyms as altLabel (a synonym of a concept (e.g. 'P&L'); the classifier and lookups use synonyms)@en, one definition (a one-sentence meaning of a concept; it becomes the facet value's description for the classifier)@en and skos:inScheme. Place it with either skos:topConceptOf (a top concept (a concept at the top of a scheme's hierarchy (skos:topConceptOf), e.g. Income evidence)) or broader (the parent link in a taxonomy hierarchy (Paystub broader Income)) (a child). Never skos:narrower: the hierarchy is stated once.
  5. ⚙ Run the taxonomy check (below).
Output
  • usecase1/taxonomy/uc1-taxonomy.seed.skos.ttl (248 triples (one RDF statement: subject – predicate – object, e.g. APP-1001 receivedAt 2026-02-06)).
⚙ Verify
powershell
uv run --no-project --with rdflib==7.6.0 python usecase1/checks/check_taxonomy.py
text
== Taxonomy (SKOS)
  info  checking uc1-taxonomy.seed.skos.ttl (seed; the VocBench export replaces it after step 1.1u)
  info  file: usecase1\taxonomy\uc1-taxonomy.seed.skos.ttl
  PASS  file parses as Turtle
  info  248 triples
  PASS  the 4 concept schemes exist
  PASS  concept ids per scheme equal the contract
  PASS  every concept has exactly one prefLabel@en and one skos:notation
  PASS  notation == IRI local name (in the uc1tx: namespace)
  PASS  hierarchy (child -> parent) equals the contract
  PASS  no key (pref, alt or hidden label, or notation) is shared by two concepts of one scheme
  PASS  every concept has a skos:definition@en
  PASS  seed: prefLabel@en and altLabel@en per concept equal the contract
== Taxonomy (SKOS): PASS (26 passed)          (trimmed: 26 PASS lines in all)
🔍 Review
  • Are labels and synonyms the names real documents carry (Form 710, 4506-C, P&L), so the classifier recognises them?
  • Is any synonym shared by two concepts? (The check rejects it within a scheme; the reviewer also looks across schemes.)
  • Are the six group concepts (Application, Income, Assets, Hardship, Tax, Property) clearly categories (definitions start “Category:”)?
  • Is every definition one sentence a classifier can use?
  • Is every notation equal to its IRI's local name, and is there no skos:narrower?
Feeds into →
1.1u loads it into VocBench once. Until the export exists, catalog-v001.xml points the core's import at it (1.2) and the checks read it (common.taxonomy_file()). The requirement files (1.4) and the golden graphs (1.6) reference its concept IRIs.
✅ Done when
  • 26 PASS lines; the SMEs signed off labels, synonyms and definitions.

Step 1.1u moves the taxonomy into its permanent home. The figure shows the one-time round trip: the seed goes in once, the export comes out, and a check proves the two hold the same content. From then on the arrow only runs one way: VocBench → export → git.

1.1uVocBench project, load the seed, export, round tripopen · action needed
Purpose
Make VocBench 3 (a web-based editor for taxonomies (SKOS); subject-matter experts maintain the controlled words here, with review before publishing) the taxonomy's source of truth. The round trip (load the seed into VocBench, export it again and prove nothing was lost or altered (check_taxonomy.py --roundtrip)) is the proof that the export can replace the seed everywhere. The seed is used exactly once: after this step, a second load would mix old seed content with SME edits.
Who
👤 Administrator (creates the project) with the SME; a second person as validator; engineer (checks, catalog, PR); reviewer.
Inputs
  • VocBench running (0.3) and the administrator login.
  • usecase1/taxonomy/uc1-taxonomy.seed.skos.ttl passing its check (1.1).
  • 0.8 done: usecase1/ committed on the branch (a separate line of work in git; Phase 1 work lives on a branch until its pull request is merged) uc1/phase1, so the export and the catalog change show up as diffs.
Do
  1. 👤 Start VocBench: run C:\Tools\vocbench3-15.1.2-full\bin\semanticturkey.bat in its own PowerShell window and wait for Started Application in … seconds.
  2. 👤 Open http://localhost:1979/vocbench3 and log in as the administrator. Leave the console open.
  3. 👤 Create the project. On the Projects page click Create and fill in:
    FieldValueWhy
    Project Nameuc1-loss-mitigationThe name the ledger and this runbook use.
    Base URI (the namespace a VocBench project mints IRIs in; ours is https://rag-os.example/uc1/taxonomy, so concept IRIs end #<notation>)https://rag-os.example/uc1/taxonomyNew concept IRIs then live in the uc1tx: namespace (…/taxonomy#), the same as the seed and the ontology's references.
    TypeSKOSA taxonomy, not an OWL ontology.
    Lexicalization (lexicalization model (how a VocBench project stores labels: plain SKOS literals (ours) or SKOS-XL label resources))SKOS (not SKOS-XL (a SKOS extension where every label is a resource of its own; we use plain SKOS labels because the checks and the converter read plain literals))Labels stay plain skos:prefLabel / skos:altLabel literals, as the checks and the converter expect.
    History (a VocBench project option that records every change with who and when; it can only be switched on when the project is created)enabledEvery change is kept with who and when. Only possible now, at creation.
    ValidationenabledEvery later edit becomes a proposal (a VocBench edit that waits for a validator; it is not part of the export until it is approved) until a validator approves it. Only possible now, at creation.
    Leave the remaining options (such as the storage) at their defaults and confirm. If you forget History or Validation, delete the still-empty project and create it again.
  4. 👤 Open the project from the Projects list; its name now shows as the current project.
  5. 👤 Load the seed (copy the seed file's concepts into the empty VocBench project, once, through Global Data Management → Load Data): Global Data Management → Load Data. Click Browse next to “RDF file” and choose <repo>\usecase1\taxonomy\uc1-taxonomy.seed.skos.ttl; format Turtle; tick Implicitly validate loaded data (a Load Data option that accepts the imported content as already approved; right for a one-time bootstrap, wrong for later edits); click Submit and wait for the confirmation.
    What happened: Semantic Turkey (the server VocBench runs on; it stores each project's data and is started by bin\semanticturkey.bat) parsed the file's 248 triples and copied them into the project's own RDF store (a database for triples; Semantic Turkey keeps each VocBench project in its own store, separate from any file) (under C:\Tools\SemanticTurkeyData\). The file in the repo is untouched and no link to it remains: nothing you do in VocBench ever writes back to the seed.
    Why implicit validation, and only now: with Validation on, every loaded triple would otherwise arrive as a pending proposal, 248 of them to approve one by one, and none would be in the export until approved. The seed was already reviewed and checked in 1.1, so this bootstrap (the one-time first fill of an empty system (here: loading the seed)) accepts it as approved. Never tick it for any later load: it would bypass the validator.
  6. 👤 Browse to confirm 4 schemes and 29 concepts. Open the Data menu. On the Scheme tab you see four schemes (Document type, Employment type, Hardship reason, Workout option). Select uc1_doc_type, then open the Concept tab: the tree shows 6 top concepts (Application, Income evidence, Asset evidence, Hardship, Tax, Property); expanding them shows 8 children (Mortgage assistance application; Paystub, Profit and loss statement, Unemployment benefit award letter; Bank statement; Hardship statement; IRS Form 4506-C; Lease agreement): 14 in this scheme. Repeat with the other schemes:
    SchemeConceptsHierarchy
    uc1_doc_type146 top concepts, 8 children
    uc1_employment_type4flat
    uc1_hardship_reason6flat
    uc1_workout_option5flat
    Total29
    Click Profit and loss statement: its details show the IRI …/taxonomy#ProfitAndLoss, notation (a concept's short code (e.g. ProfitAndLoss); here it equals the IRI's local name and the RAG-OS facet value id) ProfitAndLoss, alternative labels “P&L” and “income statement”, a definition and broader “Income evidence”.
  7. 👤 Export before any edit (Export Data (VocBench's export function (Global Data Management → Export Data): writes the project to a file, here Turtle)): Global Data Management → Export Data, with these settings:
    SettingValueWhy
    Graphs to exportthe project's working data graph only, not the imported ontologies (expected label; confirm in the dialog)The file must hold the taxonomy and nothing else; imported vocabularies would be copied into it.
    Include inferred statementsunticked (expected label; confirm in the dialog)Only what the SMEs stated. Inferred triples (e.g. skos:narrower from skos:broader) are statements nobody approved, and they add noise to every diff.
    Export transformations / filtersnone (expected; confirm in the dialog)The export must be the project's content as it is; the round trip proves that.
    FormatTurtleThe checks, Protégé's catalog and the converter read Turtle.
    Deploymentsave to fileThe browser downloads the file; git holds it.
    The browser downloads it; move it into the repo under the exact name:
    powershell
    # use the name your browser gave the download
Move-Item "$HOME\Downloads\<downloaded file>.ttl" usecase1\taxonomy\uc1-taxonomy.skos.ttl
  8. ⚙ Check the header survived. The export must still declare <https://rag-os.example/uc1/taxonomy> a owl:Ontology (the catalog maps that ontology IRI (the identifier in a file's owl:Ontology header; the catalog and Protégé find each file by it) to this file) and the SKOS declarations (they keep the closure OWL 2 DL):
    powershell
    uv run --no-project --with rdflib==7.6.0 python -c "from rdflib import Graph, URIRef; from rdflib.namespace import OWL, RDF, SKOS; g = Graph().parse('usecase1/taxonomy/uc1-taxonomy.skos.ttl'); print('ontology header:', (URIRef('https://rag-os.example/uc1/taxonomy'), RDF.type, OWL.Ontology) in g); print('SKOS classes declared:', sum((c, RDF.type, OWL.Class) in g for c in (SKOS.Concept, SKOS.ConceptScheme)), 'of 2'); print('SKOS annotation properties declared:', sum(str(s).startswith(str(SKOS)) for s in g.subjects(RDF.type, OWL.AnnotationProperty)), 'of 9')"
    text
    ontology header: True
SKOS classes declared: 2 of 2
SKOS annotation properties declared: 9 of 9
    (Output captured on the seed; the export must print the same.) If VocBench dropped them, add them back before committing: open the seed in VS Code, copy its @prefix lines and the block from <https://rag-os.example/uc1/taxonomy> a owl:Ontology ; down to dct:description a owl:AnnotationProperty . (the header and the “OWL 2 DL declarations”), and append them at the end of the export (Turtle allows prefixes anywhere before their use); change the dct:title from “(seed)” to “(VocBench export)”. Re-run the command above, and record in the pull request and in usecase1/README.md that every export needs this step.
  9. ⚙ Round trip. Compare the seed with the export:
    powershell
    uv run --no-project --with rdflib==7.6.0 python usecase1/checks/check_taxonomy.py --roundtrip
    Expect the 6 PASS lines shown under ⚙ Verify. Any “only in A / only in B” line names the concept and property that differ: fix it in VocBench (with validation), export again, re-run.
  10. 👤 Catalog switch (editing catalog-v001.xml so the taxonomy import points at the VocBench export instead of the seed). In VS Code open usecase1/ontology/catalog-v001.xml and change the taxonomy entry's uri from the seed to the export:

    xml

<uri id="uc1-taxonomy" name="https://rag-os.example/uc1/taxonomy" uri="../taxonomy/uc1-taxonomy.skos.ttl"/>
    Also update the comment above it (it says to switch after the export; now it is switched). From now on Protégé, ROBOT and the checks' catalog test use the export. (The checks pick the export automatically once it exists; check_ontology.py fails until the catalog agrees.)
  11. ⚙ Run everything:
    powershell
    uv run --no-project --with rdflib==7.6.0 --with pyshacl==0.40.1 --with owlready2 --with pyyaml python usecase1/checks/run_all.py
    The taxonomy block now checks the export (25 PASS lines: the seed-only label comparison is replaced by an info line) and the ontology block reasons over the export.

  • usecase1/ontology/catalog-v001.xml (one entry changed).

Do the export and the round trip before any edit. The round trip compares with the seed, so it is a one-time proof: once SMEs start editing, the export is expected to differ from the seed and --roundtrip will (correctly) report the differences. From then on run check_taxonomy.py without --roundtrip.

Later: adding a concept in VocBench (the right way)

Not part of 1.1u, but the first thing SMEs will want to do. Adding a concept is a change to the naming contract, because check_taxonomy.py asserts the concept ids per scheme on the export as well (labels and synonyms may change freely; ids may not change silently). Example, hypothetical: a new document type “Federal tax return” with id TaxReturn under Tax.

  1. 👤 Contract first. Add the concept to the taxonomy table of usecase1/README.md and to the contract maps at the top of usecase1/checks/check_taxonomy.py, in one pull request the KE reviews: CONTRACT (scheme → concept id → preferred label and synonyms; here "TaxReturn": ("Federal tax return", frozenset({"Form 1040"})) under uc1_doc_type), HIERARCHY (child → parent; here "TaxReturn": "Tax") and, only for a new top concept, TOP_CONCEPTS instead of HIERARCHY. Missing any of them makes the check fail on the next export.
  2. 👤 Create it in VocBench (SME, in the project): on the Concept tab select the parent (Tax) and use the create function. In the create dialog choose to specify the IRI yourself and enter https://rag-os.example/uc1/taxonomy#TaxReturn; preferred label “Federal tax return”, language en. VocBench otherwise mints a random c_… IRI, which the check rejects: the local name must equal the notation, which is the RAG-OS facet value id. IRIs are permanent; if a c_… concept was made by mistake, delete it before approval and create it again.
  3. 👤 On the new concept add skos:notation TaxReturn (plain, no language), synonyms as alternative labels @en (e.g. “Form 1040”), a one-sentence definition @en, and check that it is in scheme uc1_doc_type with broader Tax.
  4. 👤 Validate. The change is a proposal. A different person, the validator, reviews and approves it in VocBench's validation view. It is not in any export until approved.
  5. 👤 Export again (Global Data Management → Export Data, the settings of 1.1u) over usecase1/taxonomy/uc1-taxonomy.skos.ttl and run the header check of 1.1u. git diff should show the new concept's triples (plus any metadata VocBench records) and nothing else.
  6. ⚙ Run check_taxonomy.py (without --roundtrip) and run_all.py. From Phase 2 on, also regenerate taxonomy.yaml with the converter.
1.2Ontology coredone
Purpose
The formal model of the domain, the TBox (the schema part of an ontology (classes, properties, axioms), kept apart from the data (the ABox)): classes (a kind of thing in the ontology, e.g. Application), properties with domain and range, inverse pairs, functional properties, disjoint classes, value partitions and PROV-O links (each is explained in the Do list below). Precise enough that a reasoner (software that works out what logically follows from an ontology and detects contradictions (e.g. HermiT)) (HermiT) can prove it consistent and that SHACL and SQL can be checked against it.
Who
KE (author, in Protégé (a desktop editor for ontologies (OWL) from Stanford; the knowledge engineer edits the model and the requirements here) or VS Code); engineer (checks); SME (reads); reviewer.
Inputs
  • The ontology section of usecase1/README.md; design sections 7–8 (patterns, anti-patterns, competency questions (a question the model must be able to answer; written first, it keeps the model small and purposeful)).
  • The seed (1.1), which the core imports.
Do
  1. 👤 Write usecase1/ontology/uc1-core.ttl: header with ontology IRI https://rag-os.example/uc1 and version IRI (an owl:versionIRI naming one specific version of an ontology, so tools can tell versions apart) …/uc1/1.0. Add owl:imports (a statement that one ontology includes another; investor-a-2026.1.ttl imports the core, which imports the taxonomy) https://rag-os.example/uc1/taxonomy and a comment that the content is synthetic.
  2. 👤 Declare the external terms used (skos:Concept; the PROV-O (the W3C provenance vocabulary: records which guide version an evaluation used and what it generated) terms prov:Activity, prov:Entity, prov:used, prov:wasGeneratedBy; dct:title), declarations only.
  3. 👤 20 classes: the interfaces (a shared shape several classes implement, e.g. «Evidence» for every submitted document) Evidence and Citable, and 18 concrete classes in one owl:AllDisjointClasses (disjoint classes (classes that can share no member; lets the reasoner catch a borrower typed as a document)). Value partitions (a fixed set of allowed values modelled as individuals, e.g. Met / Missing / NotApplicable) PartyRole {PrimaryBorrower, CoBorrower} and Outcome {Met, Missing, NotApplicable}, all different.
  4. 👤 30 object properties (a link between two things, e.g. documents (Application → SubmittedDocument)), 29 datatype properties (a value on a thing, e.g. receivedAt (a date-time)), 2 annotation properties (onProperty, expectedValue). Each with one domain, one range, a label@en and a comment@en. 19 are functional (a property that may have at most one value; lets the reasoner catch e.g. an item that is both Met and Missing); 6 inverse pairs (two names for one link read from each end, e.g. documents / application) have comments that name the stored direction; derived properties' comments start “Derived:”.
  5. 👤 Dates: range xsd:dateTime (a date at midnight (dates are written as xsd:dateTime at 00:00:00 because OWL 2 has no xsd:date and HermiT rejects it)), names ending Date or At.
  6. 👤 Write usecase1/ontology/catalog-v001.xml: five entries mapping the core (and its version IRI), the taxonomy (to the seed for now) and the two version files to local paths.
  7. ⚙ Run the ontology check; ⚙ run ROBOT (a command-line ontology tool; we used it once to confirm the files are valid OWL 2 DL) (0.9) once for the OWL 2 DL profile; 👤 the Protégé review is its own step (1.2r).
Output
  • usecase1/ontology/uc1-core.ttl (490 triples)
  • usecase1/ontology/catalog-v001.xml (5 entries)
⚙ Verify
powershell
uv run --no-project --with rdflib==7.6.0 --with owlready2 python usecase1/checks/check_ontology.py
text

  PASS  only OWL 2 datatypes are used (no xsd:date etc.)

The check also enforces the axiom allow-list (a check that only permits agreed kinds of statements in the core, so a stray 'functional' or 'equivalent' axiom can't sneak in): the core may contain only the axiom kinds the design uses.

1.2rProtégé review: open, read, reason, practise a changeopen · action needed
Purpose
Prove with the KE's own tool what the scripts already prove: the files open in Protégé, the imports resolve through the catalog, HermiT (a reasoner bundled with Protégé: software that checks the ontology's logic for contradictions; we use it to prove the model is consistent) finds the model consistent, and the team can read and change a requirement. It also shows, safely, what Protégé does to a file when it saves.
Who
👤 KE (drives); SME (reads the requirements); reviewer (watches, records findings).
Inputs
  • Protégé 5.6.9 (0.4).
  • usecase1/ontology/investor-a-2026.1.ttl, investor-a-2025.1.ttl, uc1-core.ttl, catalog-v001.xml (1.2, 1.4).
  • 0.8 done: usecase1/ committed on uc1/phase1. Without that commit git diff shows nothing and git restore cannot undo the practice edit.
Do
  1. 👤 Start C:\Tools\Protege-5.6.9\Protege.exe.
  2. 👤 File → Open → <repo>\usecase1\ontology\investor-a-2026.1.ttl → Open. Protégé finds catalog-v001.xml (a lookup file next to the ontology files that tells Protégé (and the checks) which local file holds each imported ontology) in the same folder and resolves the imports: the 2026.1 file imports the core, the core imports the taxonomy (the seed now, the export after 1.1u). If Protégé reports a missing import or asks you to locate a file, the catalog is wrong: cancel and fix the catalog rather than browsing to a file by hand.
  3. 👤 Active ontology tab (Protégé's tab that shows the open ontology's IRI, its annotations and the ontologies it imports): the ontology IRI is https://rag-os.example/uc1/investor-a/2026.1 with title “Investor A Servicing Guide v2026.1: document requirements (synthetic)”; the imports show https://rag-os.example/uc1 directly and https://rag-os.example/uc1/taxonomy indirectly.

  5. 👤 Entities tab (Protégé's tab with sub-tabs for classes, object properties, data properties, annotation properties and individuals) → Classes: under owl:Thing you find the uc1 classes, e.g. Application, GuidelineVersion, SubmittedDocument and Requirement (shown under Citable, because Requirement ⊑ Citable), plus skos:Concept and the PROV classes.
  6. 👤 Entities → Individuals (individuals (one concrete thing in the ontology, e.g. requirement ia26:R4)): ia:InvestorA, ia:v2026.1, the requirements ia26:R1 … ia26:R8, their parameter individuals (R3-condition, R4-condition, R7-condition, R8-condition; R1-recency, R3-recency, R4-recency, R5-recency, R6-recency, R8-recency), the guide sections ia26:s4.2.1 … ia26:s4.5, the 29 taxonomy concepts (individuals of skos:Concept) and the value-partition members (Met, Missing, NotApplicable, PrimaryBorrower, CoBorrower).
  7. 👤 Read a requirement: R4. Select ia26:R4. Its property assertions (a stated value of an object or data property on one individual, e.g. R4-recency maxAgeDays 90): object properties guidelineVersion v2026.1, documentType Profit and loss statement (uc1tx:ProfitAndLoss), condition R4-condition, recency R4-recency, guideSection §4.3.2; data property requirementId “R4”. Select ia26:R4-condition: its rule parameters onProperty employmentType and expectedValue SelfEmployed appear among its annotations, not its property assertions, because they are annotation properties (the reasoner ignores them; only the SHACL template reads them). Select ia26:R4-recency: data property assertions maxAgeDays 90 and signatureRequired true. In investor-a-2025.1.ttl (open it the same way) ia25:R4-recency has only maxAgeDays 120, and there is no R8.
  8. 👤 Reason. Reasoner → HermiT, then Reasoner → Start reasoner (Start reasoner (Protégé's Reasoner menu command that runs the selected reasoner (HermiT) over the open ontology and its imports)). Success: after a few seconds the inferred hierarchy appears, nothing is red, and no dialog reports an inconsistent ontology (an ontology whose statements contradict each other; nothing inferred from it can be trusted until it is fixed). Failure looks like: a dialog saying the ontology is inconsistent (Protégé offers an explanation; read it, it names the clashing statements); or classes listed under owl:Nothing, usually in red (unsatisfiable classes (a class that cannot have any member without a contradiction; reasoners list it under owl:Nothing, usually in red)); or an error naming an unsupported datatype, which means a non-OWL-2 type such as xsd:date slipped in.
  9. 👤 Practise a change on a scratch branch:
    powershell
    git switch -c practice/protege-edit
    In Individuals select ia26:R4-recency → Property assertions → edit maxAgeDays from 90 to 30 → OK. File → Save (it keeps the Turtle format it opened).
  10. ⚙ See what the save did:
    powershell
    git diff --stat

    Besides your one value, Protégé rewrote the file: the header comments are gone, statements are re-ordered and re-grouped, prefixes may be written differently. That is why every Protégé change is reviewed as a diff and followed by the checks.
  11. ⚙ Run the checks:

    text

...

    The lesson: the checks protect exactly what the golden cases cover. A change from 90 to 60 days would have passed every check: re-running the golden cases with 60 changes no expected answer. A real rule change therefore always adds a golden case on the new boundary (section 13, change recipes). usecase1/checks/mutation_tests.py keeps this exact 30-day mutation as an automatic test (1.6).
  12. 👤 Revert: go back to the branch you came from (uc1/phase1); the uncommitted edit travels with you, and git restore discards it there:

git status

    git status must show no modified file under usecase1/ontology. Run run_all.py again: ALL PASS.

Output

  • Did HermiT run on both version files without errors?
  • Did the practice edit fail the golden check as expected, and was it fully reverted?
  • Is the formatting policy written down?
  • What NOT to do (check none happened): add a class per guide version (e.g. a ProfitAndLoss2026 class; versions are data, and the check “no version-specific uc1 names” rejects it); declare uc1 classes or properties in a version file; save as RDF/XML or OWL/XML (the checks read Turtle only; in File → Save as always keep Turtle); type a date as xsd:date; edit the taxonomy in Protégé (it belongs to VocBench).

1.3Semantic model DDL: create and drop scriptsdone
Purpose
The facts of each case in business terms, each stored once, with no rules: a new guide version never needs a schema change. Standalone scripts, not product migrations, so the tables can be removed in one step.
Who
Engineer (author); KE (column ↔ property mapping); reviewer.
Inputs
  • The semantic-model table in usecase1/README.md; the core (1.2) for names.
Do
  1. 👤 Write usecase1/sql/uc1_create.sql: 11 uc1_* tables; types TEXT, INTEGER, DOUBLE PRECISION, BOOLEAN, DATE, TIMESTAMP only; TEXT ids; NOT NULL primary keys; CHECKs (role, outcome, method, confidence 0–1, booleans IN (TRUE, FALSE), two-letter state); UNIQUE constraints; two partial unique indexes (one primary party per application; one open-ended guide version per investor); an index for every foreign key; and deliberately no IF NOT EXISTS, so a second run fails loudly.
  2. 👤 Write usecase1/sql/uc1_drop.sql: one DROP TABLE IF EXISTS per table, children first, so it needs no CASCADE and is safe to run twice.
  3. ⚙ Run the SQL check. It builds a fresh SQLite (a single-file database built into Python; the local home of the uc1_* tables (.data/uc1.db)) database .data/uc1.db and runs a static lint (a check that reads a file's text for forbidden patterns without running it). Then it compares every column with the contract, inserts a positive control (a valid example that must be accepted; proves a check rejects bad data for the right reason, not because it rejects everything) and tries each violation (each must be rejected with its expected message). Finally it plants violations for every invariant query (a SQL query that must return no rows on valid data (e.g. two primary borrowers on one application); any row it returns is a violation) and proves the drop script.
Output
  • usecase1/sql/uc1_create.sql
  • usecase1/sql/uc1_drop.sql
  • .data/uc1.db (rebuilt by the check; git-ignored)
⚙ Verify
powershell
uv run --no-project python usecase1/checks/check_sql.py
text
== Semantic model (uc1_create.sql / uc1_drop.sql)
  PASS  uc1_create.sql has no non-portable syntax (static lint)
  PASS  fresh uc1.db created from uc1_create.sql (foreign_keys ON)
  PASS  uc1_create.sql fails loudly on an existing schema (table uc1_investor already exists)
  PASS  table set matches the contract (11 tables)
  PASS  columns per table match the contract (names and order)
  PASS  every column's declared type matches the contract
  PASS  no generic or derived column (status, value, resolved_*, *status*, age_at_receipt_days, months_before_receipt, governing_version_id, satisfied_by_document_id)
  PASS  every FK column leads a full index (11 FKs, autoindexes count)
  PASS  positive control: a valid graph (incl. document, item and item-document link) inserts
  PASS  rejected: two primary parties on one application (UNIQUE constraint failed: uc1_application_party.application_id)
  PASS  rejected: 'human' classification with no reviewer (CHECK constraint failed: classification_method <> 'human' OR classification_reviewed_by IS NOT NULL)
  PASS  invariant query detects a planted violation: an investor's guide versions do not overlap in time
  PASS  uc1_drop.sql drops each table only after every table referencing it
  PASS  uc1_drop.sql is idempotent (runs again on an empty schema)
  ...
== Semantic model (uc1_create.sql / uc1_drop.sql): PASS (68 passed)          (trimmed: 68 PASS lines in all)

--postgres is a placeholder: it adds FAIL PostgreSQL proof is step 1.7b: not run. It is not a gate item; the evidence for PostgreSQL is the captured psql output of 1.7b.

  • Is every *_date column DATE and every *_at TIMESTAMP (UTC)?
  • Is personal data minimal (only display_name; tax_id_last4 was dropped in review)?
  • Can one evaluation item be satisfied by several documents (uc1_evaluation_item_document; R5 needs two bank statements)?

1.4Requirements per guide versiondone
Purpose
Each guide version (one published version of an investor's servicing guide, with effective dates)'s rules as data, one requirements file (one Turtle file per guide version (investor-a-<version>.ttl) holding that version's requirements as data) per version, side by side: a new version is a new file, never a schema change and never a new class. Each requirement (one rule of a guide version: which document, when it applies, how recent, which section) is an n-ary relation (a relationship among more than two things, modelled as its own object (a Requirement links version, document type, condition, recency and section)) linking the version, a document type, an optional condition, an optional recency rule (how recent, signed or how many documents a requirement needs (maxAgeDays, signatureRequired, minCount, mostRecentMonths)) and a guide section.
Who
KE (author, in Protégé or VS Code); SME (content, sign-off); reviewer.
Inputs
  • The core (1.2); the taxonomy concepts (1.1); the synthetic guide wording (design section 7).
Do
  1. 👤 Write usecase1/ontology/investor-a-2025.1.ttl: ontology IRI …/uc1/investor-a/2025.1 importing the core; ia:InvestorA; ia:v2025.1 (versionLabel, the effective dates (a guide version's effectiveFromDate and effectiveToDate, both inclusive; no end date means open-ended) effectiveFromDate 2025-01-01T00:00:00 and effectiveToDate 2025-12-31T00:00:00, issuedBy). Guide sections ia25:s… with sectionNumber and rdfs:seeAlso (a link to where more information lives; each GuideSection points at its section of the guide) <guide://…>. Requirements ia25:R1 … R7 with a label that cites the section, requirementId, guidelineVersion, documentType (a taxonomy concept), optional condition (onProperty / expectedValue) and optional recency.
  2. 👤 Write usecase1/ontology/investor-a-2026.1.ttl from it with namespace ia26:, open-ended from 2026-01-01: R4's recency tightened (120 days → 90 days and signed) and R8 added (an unemployment benefit award letter within 60 days when the hardship reason is Unemployment). R-ids stay stable across versions.
  3. 👤 Add both files to catalog-v001.xml (already there today).
  4. ⚙ Run the ontology check (version-file lines below) and the golden run (1.6).
Output
  • usecase1/ontology/investor-a-2025.1.ttl (144 triples)
  • usecase1/ontology/investor-a-2026.1.ttl (167 triples)

text

Phase 4 matches each Markdown heading of the ingested guide to a GuideSection's sectionNumber + label (an exact match on the heading's last segment), so its citations are section-level, not page-level: Markdown has no pages. The rdfs:seeAlso link (a placeholder guide://…#section-… address) records which section that is; it is not resolved to a page.

1.5SHACL completeness template and data-quality shapesdone
Purpose
One generic rule, the completeness template (the single SHACL-SPARQL rule (uc1-requirements.shapes.ttl) that checks every requirement of the governing version) written in SHACL (the W3C language for rules a data graph must satisfy; the validation report lists each violation), turns requirement data into missing items. It works under the closed world (SHACL's assumption that the data is complete as given, which is what a completeness check needs) assumption, which OWL cannot do (it is open world (OWL's assumption that anything not stated may still be true, so OWL alone can't say a document is missing)). Separate data-quality shapes (a SHACL rule about the data itself (e.g. every application has a received date); its failures are fixed by the servicer, never sent to a borrower) catch defects in the data itself, so a data problem is never sent to a borrower as a missing document.
Who
Engineer (only; SMEs never edit SHACL); KE (consulted); reviewer.
Inputs
  • The core (1.2); the requirement files (1.4); the derived value (a value computed from stored facts when needed (document age, governing version), never stored itself) rules in usecase1/README.md.
Do
  1. 👤 Write usecase1/ontology/uc1-requirements.shapes.ttl: one shape (one SHACL rule set with a target, e.g. 'every Application must satisfy its requirements') uc1shape:DocumentRequirement targeting every uc1:Application (the focus node (the thing a SHACL shape checks; here every uc1:Application)). It has one SHACL-SPARQL (a SHACL constraint written as a SPARQL query; every row the query returns is one violation) constraint, i.e. a SPARQL (the query language for RDF; our SHACL template is written as a SPARQL query) query: for the application's governingVersion it returns one row per requirement whose conditions all hold (on the application or one of its parties) and whose satisfying documents (the type or a narrower one; age ≤ maxAgeDays; signed if required; distinct months within mostRecentMonths) number fewer than minCount (default 1). The message is the requirement's own label.
  2. 👤 Write usecase1/ontology/uc1-dataquality.shapes.ttl: SHACL Core (the basic part of SHACL (cardinality, datatype, value checks); our data-quality shapes use only this) shapes, e.g. an application has exactly one receivedAt, one governingVersion, one hardship reason from the taxonomy and at least one party.
  3. ⚙ Run the golden case G01 (the worked example) through pySHACL (a Python library that checks a data graph against SHACL rules and reports every violation; it is the completeness engine's rule runner); the full golden run (1.6) is the template's real test.
Output
  • usecase1/ontology/uc1-requirements.shapes.ttl
  • usecase1/ontology/uc1-dataquality.shapes.ttl
⚙ Verify

1.6Golden cases G01–G11done
Purpose
Hand-written test applications with the answer fixed in advance by the compliance view. A golden case (a hand-written test application with the answer fixed in advance by the compliance view) is never generated by the engine, so it can judge the engine: in Phase 2 these are the acceptance tests.
Who
Engineer (graphs); SME and compliance (scenarios and expected answers); reviewer.
Inputs
  • The requirement files (1.4); the shapes (1.5); the worked example (design section 10).
Do
  1. 👤 For each scenario write usecase1/golden/Gnn.ttl: app:APP-10nn with receivedAt, hardshipReason, a loan owned by ia:InvestorA, parties (role, employment type, rental income) and documents (received, classification, document date, period, signature), with the derived values written out (ageAtReceiptDays, monthsBeforeReceipt). Do not state governingVersion: the runner adds it once per guide version.
  2. 👤 Write usecase1/golden/expected.yaml (the expected.yaml (the file listing each golden case's expected missing requirement ids per guide version)): per case the application, title, received_at, governs and the missing ids per version, worked out by hand from the requirements.
  3. ⚙ Golden run (evaluating every golden case under every guide version and comparing with the expected missing items (run_golden.py)): every case under both versions; ⚙ check_consistency.py recomputes the derived values from the dates.
  4. ⚙ Mutation tests (changing one fact on purpose and checking the result changes as it should; proves a test is not passing by accident): usecase1/checks/mutation_tests.py changes one fact at a time, in memory (nothing on disk changes), and asserts the answer changes as designed: e.g. unsign G04's P&L → R4 missing under v2026.1 but not under v2025.1; re-type a G02 bank statement as a paystub → R5 missing; tighten R4 to 30 days → G04 @ v2026.1 missing R4. It is not part of run_all.py; run it on its own (command under ⚙ Verify).
Output

  • usecase1/golden/expected.yaml
  • usecase1/checks/mutation_tests.py (8 mutations)

1.7bPostgreSQL proofopen · action needed
Purpose
RAG-OS runs on PostgreSQL (the production-grade database RAG-OS uses in Azure; we prove our SQL also runs there, in a separate uc1 schema) in Azure. This portability proof (running the same SQL scripts unchanged on a second database engine (PostgreSQL 16) to prove they do not depend on SQLite quirks) runs the unchanged create and seed scripts on PostgreSQL 16, in a separate uc1 schema (a separate PostgreSQL namespace holding only Use Case 1 tables; removable with DROP SCHEMA uc1 CASCADE) so nothing touches the product's tables, checks the row counts, proves the drop script and removes the schema again.
Who
Engineer; reviewer reads the captured output.
Inputs
  • Docker Desktop running (0.5).
  • docker-compose.yml at the repo root (service postgres, image postgres:16-alpine, user and database ragos).
  • usecase1/sql/uc1_create.sql, uc1_seed.sql, uc1_drop.sql (1.3, 1.7).
Do
  1. ⚙ Start only the database (from the repo root, where docker compose (Docker's tool that starts the services described in the repo's docker-compose.yml; Phase 1 starts only its postgres service) finds docker-compose.yml):
    powershell
    # From the repo root: docker-compose.yml lives there and defines the service "postgres" (image postgres:16-alpine,
# user ragos, database ragos). Only this one service is started; the API, worker and TEI are not needed.
docker compose up -d postgres
docker compose ps postgres          # repeat until STATUS shows "(healthy)", usually a few seconds
    The service publishes no port to the host, so we do not need a local psql: we run psql (PostgreSQL's command-line client; used inside the container to run the SQL scripts) inside the container (an isolated Linux process with its own files, run by Docker; here it runs PostgreSQL 16) with docker compose exec. Wait until the status is healthy (Docker's status for a service whose health check passes; for postgres the check is pg_isready).
  2. ⚙ Create, seed and count in one input. The prelude creates the schema and sets the search_path (PostgreSQL's list of schemas used for unqualified table names; SET search_path TO uc1 makes the scripts create and read uc1.uc1_* tables); ON_ERROR_STOP (a psql variable: stop at the first failing statement (exit code 3) instead of carrying on and hiding the error) makes psql stop at the first error instead of carrying on:
    powershell
    # 1) The row-count query, kept in a variable (a single-quoted here-string: nothing inside is expanded).
$counts = @'
SELECT table_name, row_count FROM (
              SELECT 1 AS n, 'uc1_investor' AS table_name, count(*) AS row_count FROM uc1_investor
    UNION ALL SELECT 2, 'uc1_guideline_version',  count(*) FROM uc1_guideline_version
    UNION ALL SELECT 3, 'uc1_loan',               count(*) FROM uc1_loan
    UNION ALL SELECT 4, 'uc1_borrower',           count(*) FROM uc1_borrower
    UNION ALL SELECT 5, 'uc1_application',        count(*) FROM uc1_application
    UNION ALL SELECT 6, 'uc1_application_party',  count(*) FROM uc1_application_party
    UNION ALL SELECT 7, 'uc1_submitted_document', count(*) FROM uc1_submitted_document
    UNION ALL SELECT 8, 'uc1_holiday',            count(*) FROM uc1_holiday
) AS c ORDER BY n;
'@

# 2) One input: schema prelude + create script + seed script + the counts. -Raw reads each file as one string.
$sql = @(
    'CREATE SCHEMA IF NOT EXISTS uc1; SET search_path TO uc1;'
    (Get-Content usecase1/sql/uc1_create.sql -Raw)
    (Get-Content usecase1/sql/uc1_seed.sql -Raw)
    $counts
) -join "`n"

# 3) Pipe it into psql inside the container. -T: no terminal, so stdin can be piped.
$sql | docker compose exec -T postgres psql -U ragos -d ragos -v ON_ERROR_STOP=1
"exit code: $LASTEXITCODE"          # 0 = every statement ran; 3 = psql stopped at the first error
  3. 👤 Read the output: one line per statement, then the count table. The eight counts must be exactly 1 investor, 2 guideline versions, 11 loans, 12 borrowers, 11 applications, 12 parties, 65 documents and 22 holidays.
  4. ⚙ Prove the drop script on PostgreSQL:
    powershell
    # Prove the drop script on PostgreSQL too: it must remove every uc1_ table without CASCADE.
$sql = @(
    'SET search_path TO uc1;'
    (Get-Content usecase1/sql/uc1_drop.sql -Raw)
    "SELECT count(*) AS uc1_tables_left FROM information_schema.tables WHERE table_schema = 'uc1';"
) -join "`n"
$sql | docker compose exec -T postgres psql -U ragos -d ragos -v ON_ERROR_STOP=1
  5. ⚙ Clean up:
    powershell
    # Clean-up: remove the schema (and anything left in it). The product's own tables in schema public are untouched.
'DROP SCHEMA uc1 CASCADE;' | docker compose exec -T postgres psql -U ragos -d ragos -v ON_ERROR_STOP=1
docker compose stop postgres        # optional: stop the container; its data volume (pgdata) is kept
  6. 👤 Record it: paste the outputs into the pull request; in usecase1/CHANGES.md change the row “uc1_* tables in PostgreSQL schema uc1” from planned to proven (date; schema dropped after the proof), and in usecase1/README.md change “to be proven … (step 1.7b; open)” to proven, with the date.
Output
  • Captured psql output in the pull request; usecase1/CHANGES.md row and usecase1/README.md sentence updated.
  • No lasting database change: the uc1 schema is dropped at the end.
⚙ Verify

Expected (not yet captured), from the scripts' contents and psql's standard output format:

text
CREATE SCHEMA
SET
CREATE TABLE                       <- 11 of these, one per uc1_* table
CREATE INDEX                       <- 9 of these (CREATE UNIQUE INDEX also prints CREATE INDEX)
INSERT 0 1                         <- 114 INSERT lines in all; the two multi-row inserts print
INSERT 0 2                            INSERT 0 2 (guideline versions) and INSERT 0 22 (holidays)
...
       table_name       | row_count
------------------------+-----------
 uc1_investor           |         1
 uc1_guideline_version  |         2
 uc1_loan               |        11
 uc1_borrower           |        12
 uc1_application        |        11
 uc1_application_party  |        12
 uc1_submitted_document |        65
 uc1_holiday            |        22
(8 rows)

exit code: 0
text
SET
DROP TABLE                         <- 11 of these
 uc1_tables_left
-----------------
               0
(1 row)
text
DROP SCHEMA
(if tables were still there, PostgreSQL first prints NOTICE:  drop cascades to 11 other objects, plus a DETAIL list)

If the create step fails with relation "uc1_investor" already exists, an earlier run left the schema behind: run the clean-up, then load again. That loud failure is intended: the create script has no IF NOT EXISTS.

🔍 Review
  • Did every statement run (exit code 0) with no ERROR line?
  • Do the eight counts match exactly?
  • Did the drop script leave 0 uc1 tables, without CASCADE?
  • Was the schema dropped at the end, and is the ledger row updated?
  • Note: check_sql.py --postgres still prints its placeholder FAIL; the evidence for this step is the captured output, not that flag.
Feeds into →
